Yeah, um… I guess what really made me come in is I’ve been getting really jumpy at work. I’m at the front desk, so there’s always noise in the lobby, you know—doors, people dragging bags, phones ringing, all that. Usually I’m fine with it, I mean, I’ve worked around people for years.

But now if a door slams or somebody rolls a suitcase really fast across the floor, I kind of… jump like it’s happening again. My heart just takes off, and then I’m standing there trying to act normal while it takes a minute to come back down. It sounds small when I say it, but it’s been bothering me enough that I figured I should talk to someone.

Yeah… I guess I have been. Not like I’m refusing to go to work or anything, but I’ve been taking the back hallway whenever I can.

It’s kind of silly, because it’s just the lobby, and I’ve walked through there a million times. But that spot by the counter where it happened… when I pass it, my chest gets tight, and I just want to get away from it. So I’ll go the long way around.

It’s annoying, too, because the back hallway is where they keep all these extra boxes and old signs, and someone’s always leaving carts there, so it’s not exactly convenient. But I still end up choosing that instead of walking past that part of the lobby.

I don’t really know. I mean, I’ve had some weird dreams lately, but I can’t say they’re about him or the counter exactly.

But when a door slams or somebody rolls a suitcase really fast across the lobby, it’s like my body reacts before I can think. I jump like it’s happening again, and then my heart is pounding and I’m just standing there trying to look normal.\n\nIt takes me a minute to get it back down. I hate that, especially at the front desk, because people are right there and I’m supposed to be calm and helpful.\n\nDoctor: What is the event that you think these noises might be reminding you of?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s that guest at the counter. The one who leaned over and knocked the equipment toward me. I don’t know why that part sticks so much, but it does.\n\nLike I’ll be answering a totally normal phone call, just doing my job, and out of nowhere I get this picture of him leaning over the desk again and everything coming at me. And then I’m not really thinking about the call anymore, I’m thinking about that. It’s annoying because I’ve answered phones forever, you know? I used to work at this other place where the phones never stopped ringing, and I could just tune it all out, but now… yeah. That’s the thing the noises bring back, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're reliving the moment when the guest leaned over and knocked the equipment toward you, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yeah. I mean, I don’t know if I’d say I fully feel like I’m back there, but out of nowhere I’ll get this picture of him leaning over the counter and knocking the equipment toward me.\n\nIt can happen when I’m just answering a totally normal phone call, which is ridiculous because that’s like… my whole job, you know? I’ve answered phones forever. But then that image pops in and I’m suddenly not really with the call anymore for a second. Since that guy at the front desk threatened me.\n\nIt’s weird because time’s been kind of… blurry with work and everything. I keep thinking it was just the other day, but then I look at the schedule and it’s been around two weeks. My brother asked me if I was still jumpy, actually—well, he didn’t say it like that, he just noticed I flinched when his kid dropped a toy. But yeah, it started after that incident.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain situations or places that remind you of the incident with the guest at the counter?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have been. But that spot by the counter where it happened… when I pass it, my chest gets tight, and I just want to get away from it. So I’ll go the long way around.\n\nIt’s annoying, too, because the back hallway is where they keep all these extra boxes and old signs, and someone’s always leaving carts there, so it’s not exactly convenient. But I still end up choosing that instead of walking past that part of the lobby.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any nightmares about the incident with the guest at the counter?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. Yeah… a little. I’m still going in, I’m not calling out or anything, but I’m definitely not moving like I used to.

I’ve answered phones forever. But then that image pops in and I’m suddenly not really with the call anymore for a second. Since that guy at the front desk threatened me.\n\nIt’s weird because time’s been kind of… blurry with work and everything. But yeah, it started after that incident.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain situations or places that remind you of the incident with the guest at the counter?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have been. So I’ll go the long way around.\n\nIt’s annoying, too, because the back hallway is where they keep all these extra boxes and old signs, and someone’s always leaving carts there, so it’s not exactly convenient. It’s not their fault. Then I feel bad after. I’m at the front desk, so there’s always noise in the lobby, you know—doors, people dragging bags, phones ringing, all that. Usually I’m fine with it, I mean, I’ve worked around people for years.\n\nBut now if a door slams or somebody rolls a suitcase really fast across the floor, I kind of… jump like it’s happening again. My heart just takes off, and then I’m standing there trying to act normal while it takes a minute to come back down. It sounds small when I say it, but it’s been bothering me enough that I figured I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: When you jump in response to a sudden noise, is it because it reminds you of something specific that has happened to you in the past?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, not every noise, obviously—like the printer can do its thing all day and I’m fine, mostly. But when a door slams or somebody rolls a suitcase really fast across the lobby, it’s like my body reacts before I can think. I jump like it’s happening again, and then my heart is pounding and I’m just standing there trying to look normal.\n\nIt takes me a minute to get it back down. I hate that, especially at the front desk, because people are right there and I’m supposed to be calm and helpful.\n\nDoctor: What is the event that you think these noises might be reminding you of?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s that guest at the counter. The one who leaned over and knocked the equipment toward me. I don’t know why that part sticks so much, but it does.\n\nLike I’ll be answering a totally normal phone call, just doing my job, and out of nowhere I get this picture of him leaning over the desk again and everything coming at me. And then I’m not really thinking about the call anymore, I’m thinking about that. It’s annoying because I’ve answered phones forever, you know? I used to work at this other place where the phones never stopped ringing, and I could just tune it all out, but now… yeah. That’s the thing the noises bring back, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're reliving the moment when the guest leaned over and knocked the equipment toward you, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yeah. I mean, I don’t know if I’d say I fully feel like I’m back there, but out of nowhere I’ll get this picture of him leaning over the counter and knocking the equipment toward me.\n\nIt can happen when I’m just answering a totally normal phone call, which is ridiculous because that’s like… my whole job, you know? I’ve answered phones forever. But then that image pops in and I’m suddenly not really with the call anymore for a second. It feels really vivid.\n\nDoctor: For how long have you been experiencing these jumpiness and vivid memories of the incident?\n\nPatient: About two weeks now, I think. Since that guy at the front desk threatened me.\n\nIt’s weird because time’s been kind of… blurry with work and everything. I keep thinking it was just the other day, but then I look at the schedule and it’s been around two weeks. My brother asked me if I was still jumpy, actually—well, he didn’t say it like that, he just noticed I flinched when his kid dropped a toy. But yeah, it started after that incident.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain situations or places that remind you of the incident with the guest at the counter?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have been. Not like I’m refusing to go to work or anything, but I’ve been taking the back hallway whenever I can.\n\nIt’s kind of silly, because it’s just the lobby, and I’ve walked through there a million times. But that spot by the counter where it happened… when I pass it, my chest gets tight, and I just want to get away from it. So I’ll go the long way around.\n\nIt’s annoying, too, because the back hallway is where they keep all these extra boxes and old signs, and someone’s always leaving carts there, so it’s not exactly convenient. But I still end up choosing that instead of walking past that part of the lobby.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any nightmares about the incident with the guest at the counter?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I’ve had some weird dreams lately, but I can’t say they’re about him or the counter exactly.\n\nLike last night I dreamed I was late for a shift, which is not new for me—I've had those “forgot to go to work” dreams since college, honestly. You wake up feeling guilty for something you didn’t even do. But as far as nightmares about that specific moment with the equipment and him leaning over… I don’t think so. At least not that I remember.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific activities or tasks at work that you've been avoiding since the incident with the guest at the counter?\n\nPatient: Tasks… not so much a task, exactly. I mean, I’m still doing the desk stuff, answering phones, checking people in, all that. Though the phones are their own nightmare sometimes—well, not nightmare, just people calling and asking questions that are literally on the website…\n\nBut, uh, the main thing is I’ve been avoiding walking through the lobby if I can help it. I’ll take the back hallway, even though it’s annoying and cluttered, because if I walk past that spot by the counter where it happened, my chest tightens up and I just want to get away from there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties in your relationships with family, friends, or colleagues since you started experiencing these symptoms?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, not like I’m getting into fights with anybody or anything. I’m usually pretty careful with coworkers, because at the front desk you kind of have to be, you know? You can’t just be in a mood when people are asking where the elevators are.\n\nBut lately I’ll hear myself snapping out these short answers over stupid little stuff, like someone asking me to repeat a confirmation number, and I’m like… why did I say it like that? It’s not their fault. Then I feel bad after. My coworkers haven’t really said anything, but I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your jumpiness or memories of the incident have been affecting your ability to do your job or interact with people at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little. I’m still going in, I’m not calling out or anything, but I’m definitely not moving like I used to.\n\nAt the reservation desk I’m slower—like I’ll double-check the same booking more than once, or I’ll kind of stall before dealing with whoever’s waiting. My manager actually shortened a couple of my shifts, which was embarrassing. She was nice about it, said it was “just to give me a breather,” but I know it’s because I’m not keeping up the same.\n\nAnd then with the lobby… I keep finding reasons not to cross it, even when guests need help out there. I’ll take the back way or ask someone else, which is ridiculous because it’s part of the job. It’s like I’m still technically doing my job, but I’m working around that whole area instead of just doing things normally.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or conversations with people because of your jumpiness or memories of the incident?\n\nPatient: Not conversations so much, I guess. I mean, people at work talk all day, and I still do that. You kind of have to.\n\nIt’s more the lobby part. I keep taking the back hallway whenever I can, even though it’s not really meant for that—it’s got carts and boxes and stuff sometimes. But walking past that exact spot near the counter where it happened… my chest tightens up, and I just get this feeling like I need to get away from it. So I avoid going through there if I can.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t know if I’d say constantly. Like, I’m not walking around thinking something’s about to happen every second.

I mean, at work you sort of have to notice things anyway—people coming in and out, bags left somewhere, guests getting lost, all that. My old manager used to say the front desk is “eyes on the whole room,” which always made me think of those hotel training videos where everyone’s smiling too much and pointing at exits… sorry, that’s not really what you asked.

So… no, I don’t think I’m constantly scanning for danger. I haven’t really noticed that exact thing. I notice the lobby more than I used to, maybe, but I’m not sure that’s the same as being on the lookout for threats.

Outside of work? Um… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed a specific thing like that.

I mean, I’ve had some weird dreams lately, but I can’t say they’re about him or the counter exactly.\n\nLike last night I dreamed I was late for a shift, which is not new for me—I've had those “forgot to go to work” dreams since college, honestly. You wake up feeling guilty for something you didn’t even do. But as far as nightmares about that specific moment with the equipment and him leaning over… I don’t think so. At least not that I remember.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific activities or tasks at work that you've been avoiding since the incident with the guest at the counter?\n\nPatient: Tasks… not so much a task, exactly. I mean, I’m still doing the desk stuff, answering phones, checking people in, all that. Though the phones are their own nightmare sometimes—well, not nightmare, just people calling and asking questions that are literally on the website…\n\nBut, uh, the main thing is I’ve been avoiding walking through the lobby if I can help it. I’ll take the back hallway, even though it’s annoying and cluttered, because if I walk past that spot by the counter where it happened, my chest tightens up and I just want to get away from there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties in your relationships with family, friends, or colleagues since you started experiencing these symptoms?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, not like I’m getting into fights with anybody or anything. I’m usually pretty careful with coworkers, because at the front desk you kind of have to be, you know? You can’t just be in a mood when people are asking where the elevators are.\n\nBut lately I’ll hear myself snapping out these short answers over stupid little stuff, like someone asking me to repeat a confirmation number, and I’m like… why did I say it like that? It’s not their fault. Then I feel bad after. My manager actually shortened a couple of my shifts, which was embarrassing. She was nice about it, said it was “just to give me a breather,” but I know it’s because I’m not keeping up the same.\n\nAnd then with the lobby… I keep finding reasons not to cross it, even when guests need help out there. I’ll take the back way or ask someone else, which is ridiculous because it’s part of the job. It’s like I’m still technically doing my job, but I’m working around that whole area instead of just doing things normally.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or conversations with people because of your jumpiness or memories of the incident?\n\nPatient: Not conversations so much, I guess. I mean, people at work talk all day, and I still do that. You kind of have to.\n\nIt’s more the lobby part. I keep taking the back hallway whenever I can, even though it’s not really meant for that—it’s got carts and boxes and stuff sometimes. But walking past that exact spot near the counter where it happened… my chest tightens up, and I just get this feeling like I need to get away from it. So I avoid going through there if I can.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on the lookout for potential threats or dangers in your environment?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say constantly. Like, I’m not walking around thinking something’s about to happen every second.\n\nI mean, at work you sort of have to notice things anyway—people coming in and out, bags left somewhere, guests getting lost, all that. My old manager used to say the front desk is “eyes on the whole room,” which always made me think of those hotel training videos where everyone’s smiling too much and pointing at exits… sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’m constantly scanning for danger. I haven’t really noticed that exact thing. I notice the lobby more than I used to, maybe, but I’m not sure that’s the same as being on the lookout for threats.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any specific activities outside of work because of the memories or jumpiness?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I guess a little. I mean, I’m still going to work, so it’s not like I’ve stopped functioning or anything.

But my manager did shorten a couple of my shifts recently. She said it was just to “take pressure off,” which is the kind of thing managers say when they’re trying not to make it awkward. I’ve been slower at the reservation desk, like I’ll double-check things too much or just take longer with people. And I keep finding reasons not to cross the lobby when guests need help over there.

It sounds kind of stupid saying it out loud, because crossing a lobby is literally part of the job. I used to joke that I got all my steps in just going back and forth to tell people where the elevators are. But now I’ll suddenly decide the brochures need straightening or I should answer an email first, anything to not walk over there.

So… yeah, it’s causing some problems at work, I guess. Not so much relationships, but daily life at work, yes.

I’ve answered phones forever. But then that image pops in and I’m suddenly not really with the call anymore for a second. I’ll take the back hallway, even though it’s annoying and cluttered, because if I walk past that spot by the counter where it happened, my chest tightens up and I just want to get away from there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties in your relationships with family, friends, or colleagues since you started experiencing these symptoms?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, not like I’m getting into fights with anybody or anything. I’m usually pretty careful with coworkers, because at the front desk you kind of have to be, you know? You can’t just be in a mood when people are asking where the elevators are.\n\nBut lately I’ll hear myself snapping out these short answers over stupid little stuff, like someone asking me to repeat a confirmation number, and I’m like… why did I say it like that? It’s not their fault. Then I feel bad after. My coworkers haven’t really said anything, but I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your jumpiness or memories of the incident have been affecting your ability to do your job or interact with people at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little. My manager actually shortened a couple of my shifts, which was embarrassing. She was nice about it, said it was “just to give me a breather,” but I know it’s because I’m not keeping up the same.\n\nAnd then with the lobby… I keep finding reasons not to cross it, even when guests need help out there. I’ll take the back way or ask someone else, which is ridiculous because it’s part of the job. It’s like I’m still technically doing my job, but I’m working around that whole area instead of just doing things normally.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or conversations with people because of your jumpiness or memories of the incident?\n\nPatient: Not conversations so much, I guess. I haven’t really noticed that exact thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess what really made me come in is I’ve been getting really jumpy at work. Usually I’m fine with it, I mean, I’ve worked around people for years.\n\nBut now if a door slams or somebody rolls a suitcase really fast across the floor, I kind of… jump like it’s happening again. My heart just takes off, and then I’m standing there trying to act normal while it takes a minute to come back down. It sounds small when I say it, but it’s been bothering me enough that I figured I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: When you jump in response to a sudden noise, is it because it reminds you of something specific that has happened to you in the past?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, not every noise, obviously—like the printer can do its thing all day and I’m fine, mostly. But when a door slams or somebody rolls a suitcase really fast across the lobby, it’s like my body reacts before I can think. I jump like it’s happening again, and then my heart is pounding and I’m just standing there trying to look normal.\n\nIt takes me a minute to get it back down. I hate that, especially at the front desk, because people are right there and I’m supposed to be calm and helpful.\n\nDoctor: What is the event that you think these noises might be reminding you of?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s that guest at the counter. The one who leaned over and knocked the equipment toward me. I don’t know why that part sticks so much, but it does.\n\nLike I’ll be answering a totally normal phone call, just doing my job, and out of nowhere I get this picture of him leaning over the desk again and everything coming at me. I used to work at this other place where the phones never stopped ringing, and I could just tune it all out, but now… yeah. I mean, I don’t know if I’d say I fully feel like I’m back there, but out of nowhere I’ll get this picture of him leaning over the counter and knocking the equipment toward me.\n\nIt can happen when I’m just answering a totally normal phone call, which is ridiculous because that’s like… my whole job, you know? I’ve answered phones forever. But then that image pops in and I’m suddenly not really with the call anymore for a second. It feels really vivid.\n\nDoctor: For how long have you been experiencing these jumpiness and vivid memories of the incident?\n\nPatient: About two weeks now, I think. Since that guy at the front desk threatened me.\n\nIt’s weird because time’s been kind of… blurry with work and everything. I keep thinking it was just the other day, but then I look at the schedule and it’s been around two weeks. My brother asked me if I was still jumpy, actually—well, he didn’t say it like that, he just noticed I flinched when his kid dropped a toy. But yeah, it started after that incident.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain situations or places that remind you of the incident with the guest at the counter?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have been. Not like I’m refusing to go to work or anything, but I’ve been taking the back hallway whenever I can.\n\nIt’s kind of silly, because it’s just the lobby, and I’ve walked through there a million times. So I’ll go the long way around.\n\nIt’s annoying, too, because the back hallway is where they keep all these extra boxes and old signs, and someone’s always leaving carts there, so it’s not exactly convenient. But I still end up choosing that instead of walking past that part of the lobby.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any nightmares about the incident with the guest at the counter?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I’ve had some weird dreams lately, but I can’t say they’re about him or the counter exactly.\n\nLike last night I dreamed I was late for a shift, which is not new for me—I've had those “forgot to go to work” dreams since college, honestly. You wake up feeling guilty for something you didn’t even do. But as far as nightmares about that specific moment with the equipment and him leaning over… I don’t think so. At least not that I remember.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific activities or tasks at work that you've been avoiding since the incident with the guest at the counter?\n\nPatient: Tasks… not so much a task, exactly. I mean, I’m still doing the desk stuff, answering phones, checking people in, all that. Though the phones are their own nightmare sometimes—well, not nightmare, just people calling and asking questions that are literally on the website…\n\nBut, uh, the main thing is I’ve been avoiding walking through the lobby if I can help it. I’ll take the back hallway, even though it’s annoying and cluttered, because if I walk past that spot by the counter where it happened, my chest tightens up and I just want to get away from there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties in your relationships with family, friends, or colleagues since you started experiencing these symptoms?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, not like I’m getting into fights with anybody or anything. I’m usually pretty careful with coworkers, because at the front desk you kind of have to be, you know? You can’t just be in a mood when people are asking where the elevators are.\n\nBut lately I’ll hear myself snapping out these short answers over stupid little stuff, like someone asking me to repeat a confirmation number, and I’m like… why did I say it like that? It’s not their fault. Then I feel bad after. My coworkers haven’t really said anything, but I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your jumpiness or memories of the incident have been affecting your ability to do your job or interact with people at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little. I’m still going in, I’m not calling out or anything, but I’m definitely not moving like I used to.\n\nAt the reservation desk I’m slower—like I’ll double-check the same booking more than once, or I’ll kind of stall before dealing with whoever’s waiting. My manager actually shortened a couple of my shifts, which was embarrassing. She was nice about it, said it was “just to give me a breather,” but I know it’s because I’m not keeping up the same.\n\nAnd then with the lobby… I keep finding reasons not to cross it, even when guests need help out there. I’ll take the back way or ask someone else, which is ridiculous because it’s part of the job. It’s like I’m still technically doing my job, but I’m working around that whole area instead of just doing things normally.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or conversations with people because of your jumpiness or memories of the incident?\n\nPatient: Not conversations so much, I guess. I mean, people at work talk all day, and I still do that. You kind of have to.\n\nIt’s more the lobby part. I keep taking the back hallway whenever I can, even though it’s not really meant for that—it’s got carts and boxes and stuff sometimes. But walking past that exact spot near the counter where it happened… my chest tightens up, and I just get this feeling like I need to get away from it. So I avoid going through there if I can.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on the lookout for potential threats or dangers in your environment?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say constantly. I haven’t really noticed that exact thing. I hate that, especially at the front desk, because people are right there and I’m supposed to be calm and helpful.\n\nDoctor: What is the event that you think these noises might be reminding you of?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s that guest at the counter. The one who leaned over and knocked the equipment toward me. I don’t know why that part sticks so much, but it does.\n\nLike I’ll be answering a totally normal phone call, just doing my job, and out of nowhere I get this picture of him leaning over the desk again and everything coming at me. And then I’m not really thinking about the call anymore, I’m thinking about that. It’s annoying because I’ve answered phones forever, you know? I used to work at this other place where the phones never stopped ringing, and I could just tune it all out, but now… yeah. That’s the thing the noises bring back, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're reliving the moment when the guest leaned over and knocked the equipment toward you, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yeah. I mean, I don’t know if I’d say I fully feel like I’m back there, but out of nowhere I’ll get this picture of him leaning over the counter and knocking the equipment toward me.\n\nIt can happen when I’m just answering a totally normal phone call, which is ridiculous because that’s like… my whole job, you know? I’ve answered phones forever. But then that image pops in and I’m suddenly not really with the call anymore for a second. It feels really vivid.\n\nDoctor: For how long have you been experiencing these jumpiness and vivid memories of the incident?\n\nPatient: About two weeks now, I think. Since that guy at the front desk threatened me.\n\nIt’s weird because time’s been kind of… blurry with work and everything. I keep thinking it was just the other day, but then I look at the schedule and it’s been around two weeks. My brother asked me if I was still jumpy, actually—well, he didn’t say it like that, he just noticed I flinched when his kid dropped a toy. But yeah, it started after that incident.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain situations or places that remind you of the incident with the guest at the counter?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have been. Not like I’m refusing to go to work or anything, but I’ve been taking the back hallway whenever I can.\n\nIt’s kind of silly, because it’s just the lobby, and I’ve walked through there a million times. But that spot by the counter where it happened… when I pass it, my chest gets tight, and I just want to get away from it. So I’ll go the long way around.\n\nIt’s annoying, too, because the back hallway is where they keep all these extra boxes and old signs, and someone’s always leaving carts there, so it’s not exactly convenient. But I still end up choosing that instead of walking past that part of the lobby.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any nightmares about the incident with the guest at the counter?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I’ve had some weird dreams lately, but I can’t say they’re about him or the counter exactly.\n\nLike last night I dreamed I was late for a shift, which is not new for me—I've had those “forgot to go to work” dreams since college, honestly. You wake up feeling guilty for something you didn’t even do. But as far as nightmares about that specific moment with the equipment and him leaning over… I don’t think so. At least not that I remember.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific activities or tasks at work that you've been avoiding since the incident with the guest at the counter?\n\nPatient: Tasks… not so much a task, exactly. I mean, I’m still doing the desk stuff, answering phones, checking people in, all that. Though the phones are their own nightmare sometimes—well, not nightmare, just people calling and asking questions that are literally on the website…\n\nBut, uh, the main thing is I’ve been avoiding walking through the lobby if I can help it. I’ll take the back hallway, even though it’s annoying and cluttered, because if I walk past that spot by the counter where it happened, my chest tightens up and I just want to get away from there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties in your relationships with family, friends, or colleagues since you started experiencing these symptoms?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, not like I’m getting into fights with anybody or anything. I’m usually pretty careful with coworkers, because at the front desk you kind of have to be, you know? You can’t just be in a mood when people are asking where the elevators are.\n\nBut lately I’ll hear myself snapping out these short answers over stupid little stuff, like someone asking me to repeat a confirmation number, and I’m like… why did I say it like that? It’s not their fault. Then I feel bad after. My coworkers haven’t really said anything, but I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your jumpiness or memories of the incident have been affecting your ability to do your job or interact with people at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little. I’m still going in, I’m not calling out or anything, but I’m definitely not moving like I used to.\n\nAt the reservation desk I’m slower—like I’ll double-check the same booking more than once, or I’ll kind of stall before dealing with whoever’s waiting. My manager actually shortened a couple of my shifts, which was embarrassing. She was nice about it, said it was “just to give me a breather,” but I know it’s because I’m not keeping up the same.\n\nAnd then with the lobby… I keep finding reasons not to cross it, even when guests need help out there. I’ll take the back way or ask someone else, which is ridiculous because it’s part of the job. It’s like I’m still technically doing my job, but I’m working around that whole area instead of just doing things normally.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or conversations with people because of your jumpiness or memories of the incident?\n\nPatient: Not conversations so much, I guess. I mean, people at work talk all day, and I still do that. You kind of have to.\n\nIt’s more the lobby part. I keep taking the back hallway whenever I can, even though it’s not really meant for that—it’s got carts and boxes and stuff sometimes. But walking past that exact spot near the counter where it happened… my chest tightens up, and I just get this feeling like I need to get away from it. So I avoid going through there if I can.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on the lookout for potential threats or dangers in your environment?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say constantly. Like, I’m not walking around thinking something’s about to happen every second.\n\nI mean, at work you sort of have to notice things anyway—people coming in and out, bags left somewhere, guests getting lost, all that. My old manager used to say the front desk is “eyes on the whole room,” which always made me think of those hotel training videos where everyone’s smiling too much and pointing at exits… sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’m constantly scanning for danger. I haven’t really noticed that exact thing. I notice the lobby more than I used to, maybe, but I’m not sure that’s the same as being on the lookout for threats.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any specific activities outside of work because of the memories or jumpiness?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? Um… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed a specific thing like that.\n\nI mean, I still go to the grocery store and do normal errands. Though now that I say that, I guess I’m not doing much extra lately—but I don’t know if that’s because of the incident or just because I’m tired after work. I’ve never been some big social calendar person anyway. My brother’s always the one making plans, and he’ll text like, “come watch the game,” and half the time I’m like, yeah, maybe, and then I forget to answer… but that’s kind of always been me.\n\nSo no, not a specific activity outside work that I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble falling or staying asleep at night?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I’ve never been the kind of person who falls asleep the second my head hits the pillow anyway.\n\nSome nights I’m on my phone too long, which I know is bad—my brother keeps telling me that, like he’s suddenly a sleep expert because he bought one of those sunrise alarm clocks. But, um… I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern of not falling asleep or waking up a lot because of this. At least not enough that I’d say it’s a definite problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your symptoms have been causing significant problems in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess a little. I mean, I’m still going to work, so it’s not like I’ve stopped functioning or anything.\n\nBut my manager did shorten a couple of my shifts recently. She said it was just to “take pressure off,” which is the kind of thing managers say when they’re trying not to make it awkward. I’ve been slower at the reservation desk, like I’ll double-check things too much or just take longer with people. And I keep finding reasons not to cross the lobby when guests need help over there.\n\nIt sounds kind of stupid saying it out loud, because crossing a lobby is literally part of the job. I used to joke that I got all my steps in just going back and forth to tell people where the elevators are. But now I’ll suddenly decide the brochures need straightening or I should answer an email first, anything to not walk over there.\n\nSo… yeah, it’s causing some problems at work, I guess. Not so much relationships, but daily life at work, yes.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean… I’m not sure how to separate “lost interest” from just being tired or not having a ton of free time.\n\nLike, I still watch stuff if it’s on, and I’ll still mess around with a game on my phone sometimes. I was never, like, building boats in my garage or anything. My brother keeps acting like I used to be some big outgoing person because I’d go over for football, but honestly half the time I went because he ordered wings and I didn’t want to cook.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed losing interest in things I used to enjoy. Nothing that jumps out at me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been trying to avoid thinking about or remembering the incident with the guest at the counter?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t have called it that exactly, but I guess I am.\n\nAt work I’ve been using the back hallway whenever I can. It’s kind of ridiculous because it takes longer, and half the time there’s carts or housekeeping stuff in the way—one time I almost tripped over a vacuum cord, which would’ve been a whole other problem. You can’t just be in a mood when people are asking where the elevators are.\n\nBut lately I’ll hear myself snapping out these short answers over stupid little stuff, like someone asking me to repeat a confirmation number, and I’m like… why did I say it like that? It’s not their fault. Then I feel bad after. My coworkers haven’t really said anything, but I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your jumpiness or memories of the incident have been affecting your ability to do your job or interact with people at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little. I’m still going in, I’m not calling out or anything, but I’m definitely not moving like I used to.\n\nAt the reservation desk I’m slower—like I’ll double-check the same booking more than once, or I’ll kind of stall before dealing with whoever’s waiting. My manager actually shortened a couple of my shifts, which was embarrassing. She was nice about it, said it was “just to give me a breather,” but I know it’s because I’m not keeping up the same.\n\nAnd then with the lobby… I keep finding reasons not to cross it, even when guests need help out there. I’ll take the back way or ask someone else, which is ridiculous because it’s part of the job. It’s like I’m still technically doing my job, but I’m working around that whole area instead of just doing things normally.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or conversations with people because of your jumpiness or memories of the incident?\n\nPatient: Not conversations so much, I guess. I mean, people at work talk all day, and I still do that. You kind of have to.\n\nIt’s more the lobby part. I keep taking the back hallway whenever I can, even though it’s not really meant for that—it’s got carts and boxes and stuff sometimes. But walking past that exact spot near the counter where it happened… my chest tightens up, and I just get this feeling like I need to get away from it. So I avoid going through there if I can.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on the lookout for potential threats or dangers in your environment?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say constantly. Like, I’m not walking around thinking something’s about to happen every second.\n\nI mean, at work you sort of have to notice things anyway—people coming in and out, bags left somewhere, guests getting lost, all that. My old manager used to say the front desk is “eyes on the whole room,” which always made me think of those hotel training videos where everyone’s smiling too much and pointing at exits… sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’m constantly scanning for danger. I haven’t really noticed that exact thing. I notice the lobby more than I used to, maybe, but I’m not sure that’s the same as being on the lookout for threats.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any specific activities outside of work because of the memories or jumpiness?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? Um… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed a specific thing like that.\n\nI mean, I still go to the grocery store and do normal errands. Though now that I say that, I guess I’m not doing much extra lately—but I don’t know if that’s because of the incident or just because I’m tired after work. I’ve never been some big social calendar person anyway. My brother’s always the one making plans, and he’ll text like, “come watch the game,” and half the time I’m like, yeah, maybe, and then I forget to answer… but that’s kind of always been me.\n\nSo no, not a specific activity outside work that I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble falling or staying asleep at night?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I’ve never been the kind of person who falls asleep the second my head hits the pillow anyway.\n\nSome nights I’m on my phone too long, which I know is bad—my brother keeps telling me that, like he’s suddenly a sleep expert because he bought one of those sunrise alarm clocks. But, um… I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern of not falling asleep or waking up a lot because of this. At least not enough that I’d say it’s a definite problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your symptoms have been causing significant problems in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess a little. I mean, I’m still going to work, so it’s not like I’ve stopped functioning or anything.\n\nBut my manager did shorten a couple of my shifts recently. She said it was just to “take pressure off,” which is the kind of thing managers say when they’re trying not to make it awkward. I’ve been slower at the reservation desk, like I’ll double-check things too much or just take longer with people. I used to joke that I got all my steps in just going back and forth to tell people where the elevators are. But now I’ll suddenly decide the brochures need straightening or I should answer an email first, anything to not walk over there.\n\nSo… yeah, it’s causing some problems at work, I guess. Not so much relationships, but daily life at work, yes.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I was never, like, building boats in my garage or anything. My brother keeps acting like I used to be some big outgoing person because I’d go over for football, but honestly half the time I went because he ordered wings and I didn’t want to cook.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed losing interest in things I used to enjoy. Nothing that jumps out at me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been trying to avoid thinking about or remembering the incident with the guest at the counter?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t have called it that exactly, but I guess I am.\n\nAt work I’ve been using the back hallway whenever I can.
